    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F32.9, F43.2, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally pushed me to make the appointment is… I’ve been having thoughts that really scared me. Not like I’m planning to do anything, and I don’t want to hurt myself, but I’ll catch myself thinking, if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier. And then I think, oh my God, why am I even thinking that?\n\nIt’s been upsetting enough that I felt like I shouldn’t just keep brushing it off or telling myself I’m fine. I’m usually someone who tries to manage things and keep going, but this felt different, like something I should say out loud to a doctor instead of keeping it in my own head.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two months, I’d say. Not every single minute in exactly the same way, but this whole… up-and-down stretch with my mood and my energy, that’s been going on since the trouble with the new district manager really started. That’s when I first noticed I wasn’t just having a bad week or feeling stressed for a day or two. It kind of kept carrying on, and then I’d think maybe it was settling, and then it would swing back again. So yes, roughly two months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this started because of what’s been happening with your new district manager?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it’s connected. I mean, I can’t say it was only that, because life is never that neat, but the timing lines up pretty clearly in my mind. This whole up-and-down stretch with my mood and my energy has been going on for about two months, and that’s right around when the trouble with the new district manager really started.\n\nAt first I told myself it was just workplace stress, or that I was overreacting, or that things would settle once we got used to each other. But it didn’t really pass the way normal stress usually does for me. It just kept going, and I kept noticing these shifts in myself. So yes, I do feel like that situation at work was at least the thing that set this off, or made it much worse.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you been feeling depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, especially in the mornings. Most mornings lately I wake up with this heavy, gray feeling, like the color has drained out of everything before I even get dressed for the store.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic, like I’m crying nonstop or anything, but it’s just this weight that’s there right away. Before I’ve even had coffee or looked at the day, I already feel kind of sunk down inside. And then I have to make myself get moving, because, you know, work is work and people are expecting me to show up and function. But the mood is there for a lot of the day, yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been finding that things you usually enjoy don’t feel enjoyable lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. That’s actually one of the things that makes me feel most unlike myself. I paint — or I used to paint — and I go to pottery class, and those were things I didn’t have to force. They were like… I don’t know, little pockets where I could breathe.\n\nLately I still try. I’ll set out my paints at home, or I’ll tell myself, “Just go to pottery, you’ll feel better once you’re there.” But then I get there, or I sit down with the supplies in front of me, and I just sort of stare at them. The clay is there, the brushes are there, everything is the same as it was before, but I don’t feel that little spark. I don’t get pulled into it the way I used to.\n\nIt’s not that I hate it now. It’s more like something in me doesn’t light up. And that bothers me, because those things have always been mine, you know? Like proof that I’m still myself outside of work and responsibilities. Lately they just feel flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with sleep lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one neatly. I mean, I know my sleep hasn’t felt especially restful, but I haven’t been keeping track in a very exact way — like how many hours, or how often I wake up, or anything like that.\n\nSome nights I get into bed and I’m aware that I’m not settling easily, but then other nights I must be sleeping because the night is just gone when I wake up. So… I don’t know if I’d call it a major sleep problem, exactly. I’ve noticed I don’t wake up feeling very refreshed, but I can’t say I’ve clearly noticed a pattern beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I feel a little embarrassed saying it that directly. It’s not like I’m walking around thinking, “I’m worthless,” every minute, but lately I do keep getting stuck on these little things at work and turning them into some big judgment about myself.\n\nLike, if a display sign is crooked and I notice it after customers have already been walking past it, I’ll think, “How did you miss that? What kind of supervisor doesn’t even catch a sign?” Or if one of the newer employees looks confused after I’ve explained something, instead of thinking maybe they just need another explanation, I start telling myself I must be terrible at training people. I’ll replay it later, over and over, like I’m reviewing evidence against myself.\n\nAnd I know, logically, these are small retail things. A crooked sign is not the end of the world. A trainee being confused doesn’t mean I’m useless. But emotionally, in the moment, it feels like proof that I’m failing at something I’m supposed to be good at. I’ve caught myself thinking, “You’re a useless supervisor,” which feels harsh even as I’m saying it, but that is where my mind goes lately.\n\nDoctor: When you’re away from work for a day or two, do these feelings get noticeably better?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wish they did, because then I could tell myself it’s just work wearing me down while I’m there, but even when I’m away for a day or two, my mind sort of carries it with me.\n\nLike if I’m at home, I’ll suddenly remember that display sign being crooked, and I’ll think, “Why didn’t you notice that sooner?” And it’s ridiculous because I’m not even in the store anymore, nobody is asking me about it, but I’ll still replay it. Or I’ll think about one of the trainees looking confused after I explained something, and instead of just letting it be a normal part of training someone, I start going over exactly what I said, whether I made it unclear, whether I sounded impatient, whether they think I don’t know what I’m doing.\n\nSo no, being away from work doesn’t make it disappear. It might be a little quieter because I’m not actively standing there seeing all the things I could be doing wrong, but the feeling still follows me. I’ll still end up telling myself I’m a useless supervisor over these small things, even when part of me knows they’re small.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two months, had you been feeling down or low most days for at least two years?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, no. I mean, I’ve had periods where I’ve felt low or discouraged before, like anybody, and I can get pretty hard on myself, but “most days for at least two years” feels too specific for me to honestly confirm.\n\nI don’t remember thinking of myself as being down almost every day for years and years before this recent stretch. I’d have bad weeks, or stressful periods, or times where I wasn’t exactly cheerful, but I don’t know that it was this steady thing going on for two years. I’d be guessing if I said yes to that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Not in a dramatic way like I can’t follow a conversation at all, but lately my thinking feels… slower, or foggier than it should.\n\nAt work, there are decisions I’ve made a thousand times without even thinking, and now I’ll just stand there second-guessing myself. Like choosing which endcap to reset first—normally I’d look at the floor, think about what needs attention, and just do it. Now I’ll go back and forth in my head: “Should I do the seasonal one first? No, maybe the clearance one. But if I start there, will that throw off the rest of the shift?” And it’s not some huge strategic decision, it’s just retail stuff I know how to do, but I can get stuck on it.\n\nIt happens at home too, which is what makes me feel silly. Something like picking out a blouse to wear can take longer than it should. I’ll stand in front of the closet feeling like my brain won’t just settle on one choice. I’ll put one on, then think it looks wrong, then change, then wonder why I’m making such a production out of it.\n\nSo yes, I guess trouble concentrating or thinking clearly is fair to say. It’s more like these ordinary automatic decisions have started feeling oddly difficult, and I don’t feel as sharp as I usually do.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two months, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think back honestly. I don’t know that I can point to another separate two-week period earlier in my life that felt like this in the same way.\n\nWhat I can say is that this current low, flat feeling has been going on much longer than two weeks. It’s been more like… about six months now, I think. It didn’t start as some dramatic drop all at once, at least not that I noticed. It was more like things that usually helped me feel like myself slowly stopped working.\n\nFor example, I used to go to art classes and even if I was tired or stressed, I’d come home feeling like I’d gotten a little piece of myself back. And my morning walks used to clear my head. They weren’t some big exercise routine, just something steady that made the day feel more manageable. But over these months, those things stopped feeling restorative. I’d still do them sometimes, but I wouldn’t come back feeling refreshed or lighter. It was more like I had gone through the motions and the flatness was still there waiting for me.\n\nSo before this stretch, I’m not sure. I’ve had down times, yes, but this particular kind of low feeling has really been this past six months.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can clearly say yes to that. I mean, I’ve definitely had times in my life where I was sad, or disappointed, or didn’t feel very motivated for a while, but when you say “nearly every day for at least two weeks,” I get a little stuck because I don’t want to give you an answer that sounds more certain than I really am.\n\nLooking back, I can remember stressful stretches, and times when I wasn’t enjoying things as much, but I don’t know that they were the same as this current period or that they lasted in that steady, almost-every-day way. Some of it is hard to separate from just normal life stress or being tired or having a bad patch.\n\nSo before this current period, I’d say I’m not sure. I can’t point to a definite earlier episode like that.\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Before this current stretch, I really can’t think of a clear time like that, no. I’ve had sad periods, of course, but not something I can honestly say was most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks.\n\nWhat stands out to me is more what’s been happening lately. Most mornings now I wake up and there’s this heavy, gray feeling already there, before I’ve even gotten dressed for the store. It’s like the day hasn’t even started yet, but the color has drained out of it. That’s the part that feels different to me. It isn’t just, “Oh, I’m tired,” or “I’m in a bad mood.” It’s more like I open my eyes and there it is, sitting on me.\n\nSo before this, I don’t know that I’d say yes. This current stretch is the one I can really describe that way.\n\nDoctor: Before the problems with your new district manager began, had you already been feeling depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I had. It’s a little hard to separate everything out neatly, because the situation with the new district manager definitely made things feel more noticeable and harder to manage, but the low, flat feeling was already there before that started.\n\nIt had been going on for quite a while — I’d say about six months altogether now. It wasn’t like one morning I woke up and everything had suddenly changed. It was more gradual. My usual routines, like going to art classes or taking my morning walks, just stopped giving me that little lift they used to. I’d still do them, but they didn’t feel restorative anymore. I’d come back and still feel sort of dull and weighed down, like nothing had really shifted.\n\nSo yes, before the problems with him began, I do think I was already feeling depressed most days. The work stress didn’t create it from nothing, if that makes sense. It just sort of landed on top of something that was already there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed a clear change, no. My appetite seems… mostly the same, I think. I mean, some days I don’t feel especially excited about food, but I wouldn’t say I’m eating dramatically more or less than usual.\n\nI still have my regular meals more or less out of habit. Breakfast, something at lunch, dinner — maybe not always with much enthusiasm, but I don’t think there’s been a major shift where I’m skipping meals all the time or feeling ravenous. So, no, nothing about my appetite really stands out to me lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, some days, though it isn’t the same every day. I’ve noticed there are days where I’m kind of restless more than slow — I’ll be in the living room and realize I’ve been pacing back and forth without really deciding to do it. Or I’ll look down and I’ve been picking at my cuticles again, just fussing with my hands while I’m thinking or trying to settle myself.\n\nBut then other days it’s the opposite. At work, even something simple like walking from the break room back out to the sales floor feels strangely slow, like my body isn’t quite keeping up with what I’m telling it to do. I don’t know if other people would notice it, exactly, but I notice it because it doesn’t feel like my normal pace. It’s not dramatic, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a time when you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual without feeling tired?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was a stretch like that. I don’t know if I would have called it “not needing sleep” at the time, exactly, but looking back, I was definitely running on much less sleep than usual and somehow still felt keyed up rather than worn out.\n\nI’d get home from work and instead of wanting to sit down or just make dinner and unwind, I felt like I had all this energy I had to put somewhere. I couldn’t really sit still. I’d start sketching ideas for the store — displays, little layout changes, ways to organize seasonal things — and I’d keep going much longer than I meant to. It wasn’t just a quick note or two; I’d have papers spread out, drawing shelves and signs and traffic flow like I was designing a whole new department.\n\nAnd then at home I’d start reorganizing things, too, like shelves and closets, moving items around because suddenly it seemed very important to make everything more efficient. I also signed up for extra training modules, ones I honestly didn’t really need for my job. At the time it felt productive, like I was just being motivated, but it was unusual for me.\n\nSo yes, I have had a time where I was much more energetic than normal and sleeping less, but not feeling tired in the way I would expect.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It was more like things that usually helped me feel like myself slowly stopped working.\n\nFor example, I used to go to art classes and even if I was tired or stressed, I’d come home feeling like I’d gotten a little piece of myself back. And my morning walks used to clear my head. They weren’t some big exercise routine, just something steady that made the day feel more manageable. But over these months, those things stopped feeling restorative. I’d still do them sometimes, but I wouldn’t come back feeling refreshed or lighter. It was more like I had gone through the motions and the flatness was still there waiting for me.\n\nSo before this stretch, I’m not sure. I’ve had down times, yes, but this particular kind of low feeling has really been this past six months.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can clearly say yes to that. I mean, I’ve definitely had times in my life where I was sad, or disappointed, or didn’t feel very motivated for a while, but when you say “nearly every day for at least two weeks,” I get a little stuck because I don’t want to give you an answer that sounds more certain than I really am.\n\nLooking back, I can remember stressful stretches, and times when I wasn’t enjoying things as much, but I don’t know that they were the same as this current period or that they lasted in that steady, almost-every-day way. Some of it is hard to separate from just normal life stress or being tired or having a bad patch.\n\nSo before this current period, I’d say I’m not sure. I can’t point to a definite earlier episode like that.\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Before this current stretch, I really can’t think of a clear time like that, no. I’ve had sad periods, of course, but not something I can honestly say was most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks.\n\nWhat stands out to me is more what’s been happening lately. Most mornings now I wake up and there’s this heavy, gray feeling already there, before I’ve even gotten dressed for the store. It’s like the day hasn’t even started yet, but the color has drained out of it. That’s the part that feels different to me. It isn’t just, “Oh, I’m tired,” or “I’m in a bad mood.” It’s more like I open my eyes and there it is, sitting on me.\n\nSo before this, I don’t know that I’d say yes. This current stretch is the one I can really describe that way.\n\nDoctor: Before the problems with your new district manager began, had you already been feeling depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I had. It’s a little hard to separate everything out neatly, because the situation with the new district manager definitely made things feel more noticeable and harder to manage, but the low, flat feeling was already there before that started.\n\nIt had been going on for quite a while — I’d say about six months altogether now. It wasn’t like one morning I woke up and everything had suddenly changed. It was more gradual. My usual routines, like going to art classes or taking my morning walks, just stopped giving me that little lift they used to. I’d still do them, but they didn’t feel restorative anymore. I’d come back and still feel sort of dull and weighed down, like nothing had really shifted.\n\nSo yes, before the problems with him began, I do think I was already feeling depressed most days. The work stress didn’t create it from nothing, if that makes sense. It just sort of landed on top of something that was already there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed a clear change, no. My appetite seems… mostly the same, I think. I mean, some days I don’t feel especially excited about food, but I wouldn’t say I’m eating dramatically more or less than usual.\n\nI still have my regular meals more or less out of habit. Breakfast, something at lunch, dinner — maybe not always with much enthusiasm, but I don’t think there’s been a major shift where I’m skipping meals all the time or feeling ravenous. So, no, nothing about my appetite really stands out to me lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, some days, though it isn’t the same every day. I’ve noticed there are days where I’m kind of restless more than slow — I’ll be in the living room and realize I’ve been pacing back and forth without really deciding to do it. Or I’ll look down and I’ve been picking at my cuticles again, just fussing with my hands while I’m thinking or trying to settle myself.\n\nBut then other days it’s the opposite. At work, even something simple like walking from the break room back out to the sales floor feels strangely slow, like my body isn’t quite keeping up with what I’m telling it to do. I don’t know if other people would notice it, exactly, but I notice it because it doesn’t feel like my normal pace. It’s not dramatic, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a time when you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual without feeling tired?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was a stretch like that. I don’t know if I would have called it “not needing sleep” at the time, exactly, but looking back, I was definitely running on much less sleep than usual and somehow still felt keyed up rather than worn out.\n\nI’d get home from work and instead of wanting to sit down or just make dinner and unwind, I felt like I had all this energy I had to put somewhere. I couldn’t really sit still. I’d start sketching ideas for the store — displays, little layout changes, ways to organize seasonal things — and I’d keep going much longer than I meant to. It wasn’t just a quick note or two; I’d have papers spread out, drawing shelves and signs and traffic flow like I was designing a whole new department.\n\nAnd then at home I’d start reorganizing things, too, like shelves and closets, moving items around because suddenly it seemed very important to make everything more efficient. I also signed up for extra training modules, ones I honestly didn’t really need for my job. At the time it felt productive, like I was just being motivated, but it was unusual for me.\n\nSo yes, I have had a time where I was much more energetic than normal and sleeping less, but not feeling tired in the way I would expect.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not serious problems like… I didn’t lose my job or get written up, and I wasn’t getting into legal trouble or spending money in some disastrous way. I was still showing up to work, still doing the basics, still taking care of myself at home. So in that sense, no, it didn’t completely derail things.

But it did start to affect how I was functioning at work, more than I wanted to admit at the time. I remember avoiding extra conversations with staff because I didn’t trust myself to be measured or make sense, if that makes sense. Like if someone wanted to talk through a scheduling issue or ask my opinion about something on the floor, I’d keep it shorter than I normally would, or I’d sort of sidestep it and say we’d come back to it later.

And I put off simple store decisions — things that normally I’d just handle without overthinking — because I wasn’t sure my judgment was right. I’d think, “Is this actually a good idea, or am I just too wound up and convinced everything needs changing?” So it wasn’t catastrophic, but it wasn’t nothing either. It made me less confident and a bit less effective, especially as a supervisor.

Before I’ve even had coffee or looked at the day, I already feel kind of sunk down inside. And then I have to make myself get moving, because, you know, work is work and people are expecting me to show up and function. But the mood is there for a lot of the day, yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been finding that things you usually enjoy don’t feel enjoyable lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. That’s actually one of the things that makes me feel most unlike myself. I paint — or I used to paint — and I go to pottery class, and those were things I didn’t have to force. They were like… I don’t know, little pockets where I could breathe.\n\nLately I still try. The clay is there, the brushes are there, everything is the same as it was before, but I don’t feel that little spark. I don’t get pulled into it the way I used to.\n\nIt’s not that I hate it now. I mean, I know my sleep hasn’t felt especially restful, but I haven’t been keeping track in a very exact way — like how many hours, or how often I wake up, or anything like that.\n\nSome nights I get into bed and I’m aware that I’m not settling easily, but then other nights I must be sleeping because the night is just gone when I wake up. So… I don’t know if I’d call it a major sleep problem, exactly. I’ve noticed I don’t wake up feeling very refreshed, but I can’t say I’ve clearly noticed a pattern beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I feel a little embarrassed saying it that directly. What kind of supervisor doesn’t even catch a sign?” Or if one of the newer employees looks confused after I’ve explained something, instead of thinking maybe they just need another explanation, I start telling myself I must be terrible at training people. I’ll replay it later, over and over, like I’m reviewing evidence against myself.\n\nAnd I know, logically, these are small retail things. A crooked sign is not the end of the world. A trainee being confused doesn’t mean I’m useless. But emotionally, in the moment, it feels like proof that I’m failing at something I’m supposed to be good at. I’ve caught myself thinking, “You’re a useless supervisor,” which feels harsh even as I’m saying it, but that is where my mind goes lately.\n\nDoctor: When you’re away from work for a day or two, do these feelings get noticeably better?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wish they did, because then I could tell myself it’s just work wearing me down while I’m there, but even when I’m away for a day or two, my mind sort of carries it with me.\n\nLike if I’m at home, I’ll suddenly remember that display sign being crooked, and I’ll think, “Why didn’t you notice that sooner?” And it’s ridiculous because I’m not even in the store anymore, nobody is asking me about it, but I’ll still replay it. Or I’ll think about one of the trainees looking confused after I explained something, and instead of just letting it be a normal part of training someone, I start going over exactly what I said, whether I made it unclear, whether I sounded impatient, whether they think I don’t know what I’m doing.\n\nSo no, being away from work doesn’t make it disappear. It might be a little quieter because I’m not actively standing there seeing all the things I could be doing wrong, but the feeling still follows me. I’ll still end up telling myself I’m a useless supervisor over these small things, even when part of me knows they’re small.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two months, had you been feeling down or low most days for at least two years?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, no. I mean, I’ve had periods where I’ve felt low or discouraged before, like anybody, and I can get pretty hard on myself, but “most days for at least two years” feels too specific for me to honestly confirm.\n\nI don’t remember thinking of myself as being down almost every day for years and years before this recent stretch. I’d have bad weeks, or stressful periods, or times where I wasn’t exactly cheerful, but I don’t know that it was this steady thing going on for two years. I’d be guessing if I said yes to that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Not in a dramatic way like I can’t follow a conversation at all, but lately my thinking feels… slower, or foggier than it should.\n\nAt work, there are decisions I’ve made a thousand times without even thinking, and now I’ll just stand there second-guessing myself. Like choosing which endcap to reset first—normally I’d look at the floor, think about what needs attention, and just do it. Now I’ll go back and forth in my head: “Should I do the seasonal one first? No, maybe the clearance one. But if I start there, will that throw off the rest of the shift?” And it’s not some huge strategic decision, it’s just retail stuff I know how to do, but I can get stuck on it.\n\nIt happens at home too, which is what makes me feel silly. Something like picking out a blouse to wear can take longer than it should. I’ll stand in front of the closet feeling like my brain won’t just settle on one choice. I’ll put one on, then think it looks wrong, then change, then wonder why I’m making such a production out of it.\n\nSo yes, I guess trouble concentrating or thinking clearly is fair to say. It’s more like these ordinary automatic decisions have started feeling oddly difficult, and I don’t feel as sharp as I usually do.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two months, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think back honestly. I don’t know that I can point to another separate two-week period earlier in my life that felt like this in the same way.\n\nWhat I can say is that this current low, flat feeling has been going on much longer than two weeks. It’s been more like… about six months now, I think. It didn’t start as some dramatic drop all at once, at least not that I noticed. It was more like things that usually helped me feel like myself slowly stopped working.\n\nFor example, I used to go to art classes and even if I was tired or stressed, I’d come home feeling like I’d gotten a little piece of myself back. And my morning walks used to clear my head. They weren’t some big exercise routine, just something steady that made the day feel more manageable. But over these months, those things stopped feeling restorative. I’d still do them sometimes, but I wouldn’t come back feeling refreshed or lighter. It was more like I had gone through the motions and the flatness was still there waiting for me.\n\nSo before this stretch, I’m not sure. I’ve had down times, yes, but this particular kind of low feeling has really been this past six months.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can clearly say yes to that. I mean, I’ve definitely had times in my life where I was sad, or disappointed, or didn’t feel very motivated for a while, but when you say “nearly every day for at least two weeks,” I get a little stuck because I don’t want to give you an answer that sounds more certain than I really am.\n\nLooking back, I can remember stressful stretches, and times when I wasn’t enjoying things as much, but I don’t know that they were the same as this current period or that they lasted in that steady, almost-every-day way. Some of it is hard to separate from just normal life stress or being tired or having a bad patch.\n\nSo before this current period, I’d say I’m not sure. I can’t point to a definite earlier episode like that.\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Before this current stretch, I really can’t think of a clear time like that, no. I’ve had sad periods, of course, but not something I can honestly say was most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks.\n\nWhat stands out to me is more what’s been happening lately. Most mornings now I wake up and there’s this heavy, gray feeling already there, before I’ve even gotten dressed for the store. It’s like the day hasn’t even started yet, but the color has drained out of it. That’s the part that feels different to me. It isn’t just, “Oh, I’m tired,” or “I’m in a bad mood.” It’s more like I open my eyes and there it is, sitting on me.\n\nSo before this, I don’t know that I’d say yes. This current stretch is the one I can really describe that way.\n\nDoctor: Before the problems with your new district manager began, had you already been feeling depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I had. It’s a little hard to separate everything out neatly, because the situation with the new district manager definitely made things feel more noticeable and harder to manage, but the low, flat feeling was already there before that started.\n\nIt had been going on for quite a while — I’d say about six months altogether now. It wasn’t like one morning I woke up and everything had suddenly changed. It was more gradual. My usual routines, like going to art classes or taking my morning walks, just stopped giving me that little lift they used to. I’d still do them, but they didn’t feel restorative anymore. I’d come back and still feel sort of dull and weighed down, like nothing had really shifted.\n\nSo yes, before the problems with him began, I do think I was already feeling depressed most days. The work stress didn’t create it from nothing, if that makes sense. It just sort of landed on top of something that was already there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed a clear change, no. My appetite seems… mostly the same, I think. I mean, some days I don’t feel especially excited about food, but I wouldn’t say I’m eating dramatically more or less than usual.\n\nI still have my regular meals more or less out of habit. Breakfast, something at lunch, dinner — maybe not always with much enthusiasm, but I don’t think there’s been a major shift where I’m skipping meals all the time or feeling ravenous. So, no, nothing about my appetite really stands out to me lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, some days, though it isn’t the same every day. I’ve noticed there are days where I’m kind of restless more than slow — I’ll be in the living room and realize I’ve been pacing back and forth without really deciding to do it. Or I’ll look down and I’ve been picking at my cuticles again, just fussing with my hands while I’m thinking or trying to settle myself.\n\nBut then other days it’s the opposite. At work, even something simple like walking from the break room back out to the sales floor feels strangely slow, like my body isn’t quite keeping up with what I’m telling it to do. I don’t know if other people would notice it, exactly, but I notice it because it doesn’t feel like my normal pace. It’s not dramatic, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a time when you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual without feeling tired?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was a stretch like that. I don’t know if I would have called it “not needing sleep” at the time, exactly, but looking back, I was definitely running on much less sleep than usual and somehow still felt keyed up rather than worn out.\n\nI’d get home from work and instead of wanting to sit down or just make dinner and unwind, I felt like I had all this energy I had to put somewhere. I couldn’t really sit still. I’d start sketching ideas for the store — displays, little layout changes, ways to organize seasonal things — and I’d keep going much longer than I meant to. It wasn’t just a quick note or two; I’d have papers spread out, drawing shelves and signs and traffic flow like I was designing a whole new department.\n\nAnd then at home I’d start reorganizing things, too, like shelves and closets, moving items around because suddenly it seemed very important to make everything more efficient. I also signed up for extra training modules, ones I honestly didn’t really need for my job. At the time it felt productive, like I was just being motivated, but it was unusual for me.\n\nSo yes, I have had a time where I was much more energetic than normal and sleeping less, but not feeling tired in the way I would expect.\n\nDoctor: During that unusually energetic period, did it cause serious problems for you at work, socially, financially, or legally?\n\nPatient: Not serious problems like… I didn’t lose my job or get written up, and I wasn’t getting into legal trouble or spending money in some disastrous way. I was still showing up to work, still doing the basics, still taking care of myself at home. So in that sense, no, it didn’t completely derail things.\n\nBut it did start to affect how I was functioning at work, more than I wanted to admit at the time. I remember avoiding extra conversations with staff because I didn’t trust myself to be measured or make sense, if that makes sense. Like if someone wanted to talk through a scheduling issue or ask my opinion about something on the floor, I’d keep it shorter than I normally would, or I’d sort of sidestep it and say we’d come back to it later.\n\nAnd I put off simple store decisions — things that normally I’d just handle without overthinking — because I wasn’t sure my judgment was right. I’d think, “Is this actually a good idea, or am I just too wound up and convinced everything needs changing?” So it wasn’t catastrophic, but it wasn’t nothing either. What kind of supervisor doesn’t even catch a sign?” Or if one of the newer employees looks confused after I’ve explained something, instead of thinking maybe they just need another explanation, I start telling myself I must be terrible at training people. I’ll replay it later, over and over, like I’m reviewing evidence against myself.\n\nAnd I know, logically, these are small retail things. A crooked sign is not the end of the world. A trainee being confused doesn’t mean I’m useless. But emotionally, in the moment, it feels like proof that I’m failing at something I’m supposed to be good at. I’ve caught myself thinking, “You’re a useless supervisor,” which feels harsh even as I’m saying it, but that is where my mind goes lately.\n\nDoctor: When you’re away from work for a day or two, do these feelings get noticeably better?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wish they did, because then I could tell myself it’s just work wearing me down while I’m there, but even when I’m away for a day or two, my mind sort of carries it with me.\n\nLike if I’m at home, I’ll suddenly remember that display sign being crooked, and I’ll think, “Why didn’t you notice that sooner?” And it’s ridiculous because I’m not even in the store anymore, nobody is asking me about it, but I’ll still replay it. But over these months, those things stopped feeling restorative. I’d still do them sometimes, but I wouldn’t come back feeling refreshed or lighter. It wasn’t like one morning I woke up and everything had suddenly changed. It was more gradual. My usual routines, like going to art classes or taking my morning walks, just stopped giving me that little lift they used to. I’d still do them, but they didn’t feel restorative anymore. I’d come back and still feel sort of dull and weighed down, like nothing had really shifted.\n\nSo yes, before the problems with him began, I do think I was already feeling depressed most days. The work stress didn’t create it from nothing, if that makes sense. Breakfast, something at lunch, dinner — maybe not always with much enthusiasm, but I don’t think there’s been a major shift where I’m skipping meals all the time or feeling ravenous. So, no, nothing about my appetite really stands out to me lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, some days, though it isn’t the same every day. I’ve noticed there are days where I’m kind of restless more than slow — I’ll be in the living room and realize I’ve been pacing back and forth without really deciding to do it. Or I’ll look down and I’ve been picking at my cuticles again, just fussing with my hands while I’m thinking or trying to settle myself.\n\nBut then other days it’s the opposite. At work, even something simple like walking from the break room back out to the sales floor feels strangely slow, like my body isn’t quite keeping up with what I’m telling it to do. I don’t know if other people would notice it, exactly, but I notice it because it doesn’t feel like my normal pace. It’s not dramatic, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a time when you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual without feeling tired?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was a stretch like that. I don’t know if I would have called it “not needing sleep” at the time, exactly, but looking back, I was definitely running on much less sleep than usual and somehow still felt keyed up rather than worn out.\n\nI’d get home from work and instead of wanting to sit down or just make dinner and unwind, I felt like I had all this energy I had to put somewhere. I couldn’t really sit still. I’d start sketching ideas for the store — displays, little layout changes, ways to organize seasonal things — and I’d keep going much longer than I meant to. It wasn’t just a quick note or two; I’d have papers spread out, drawing shelves and signs and traffic flow like I was designing a whole new department.\n\nAnd then at home I’d start reorganizing things, too, like shelves and closets, moving items around because suddenly it seemed very important to make everything more efficient. I also signed up for extra training modules, ones I honestly didn’t really need for my job. At the time it felt productive, like I was just being motivated, but it was unusual for me.\n\nSo yes, I have had a time where I was much more energetic than normal and sleeping less, but not feeling tired in the way I would expect.\n\nDoctor: During that unusually energetic period, did it cause serious problems for you at work, socially, financially, or legally?\n\nPatient: Not serious problems like… I didn’t lose my job or get written up, and I wasn’t getting into legal trouble or spending money in some disastrous way. I was still showing up to work, still doing the basics, still taking care of myself at home. So in that sense, no, it didn’t completely derail things.\n\nBut it did start to affect how I was functioning at work, more than I wanted to admit at the time. I remember avoiding extra conversations with staff because I didn’t trust myself to be measured or make sense, if that makes sense. Like if someone wanted to talk through a scheduling issue or ask my opinion about something on the floor, I’d keep it shorter than I normally would, or I’d sort of sidestep it and say we’d come back to it later.\n\nAnd I put off simple store decisions — things that normally I’d just handle without overthinking — because I wasn’t sure my judgment was right. I’d think, “Is this actually a good idea, or am I just too wound up and convinced everything needs changing?” So it wasn’t catastrophic, but it wasn’t nothing either. It made me less confident and a bit less effective, especially as a supervisor.\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did that unusually energetic, less-sleep period last?\n\nPatient: It’s a little hard to pin down as one clean block of days, because it didn’t feel like it had a sharp start and stop. The whole up-and-down stretch with my mood and energy has been going on for about two months now, really since the trouble with the new district manager started getting worse.\n\nWithin that, the higher-energy, sleeping-less part seemed to come in runs, but I don’t want to give you an exact number and make it sound more precise than it was. It wasn’t just one odd night, though. It was part of this longer couple-month period where I’ve felt like my system keeps swinging around more than usual.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I wish they did, because then I could tell myself it’s just work wearing me down while I’m there, but even when I’m away for a day or two, my mind sort of carries it with me.\n\nLike if I’m at home, I’ll suddenly remember that display sign being crooked, and I’ll think, “Why didn’t you notice that sooner?” And it’s ridiculous because I’m not even in the store anymore, nobody is asking me about it, but I’ll still replay it. I’d think, “Is this actually a good idea, or am I just too wound up and convinced everything needs changing?” So it wasn’t catastrophic, but it wasn’t nothing either. It made me less confident and a bit less effective, especially as a supervisor.\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did that unusually energetic, less-sleep period last?\n\nPatient: It’s a little hard to pin down as one clean block of days, because it didn’t feel like it had a sharp start and stop. It wasn’t just one odd night, though. It was part of this longer couple-month period where I’ve felt like my system keeps swinging around more than usual.\n\nDoctor: Did that unusually energetic, needing-less-sleep period ever last 4 days in a row or longer?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure I can separate it cleanly into, like, exactly four days versus three days or five days. The way I’ve been thinking about it is more that this whole up-and-down stretch with my mood and energy has been going on for about two months now, since the trouble with the new district manager really started.\n\nSo… yes, it wasn’t just a day here or there, but I’d hesitate to say I tracked it closely enough to swear to a specific number of days in a row. It’s been more like this longer period where my energy and sleep have been off and kind of shifting around."
